 ## MVP JSX Transpiler in C++ — Summary

### What It Is
Source-to-source transpiler: **JSX** → **JS with `React.createElement` calls**. No source maps, no compiler optimizations, no Svelte-level complexity.

### Core Architecture

| Component | Responsibility |
|-----------|---------------|
| **Lexer** | Tokenize JS + JSX tokens. Handle context: `<` is "less than" in statements, JSX opener in expressions |
| **Parser** | Recursive descent. JS grammar with JSX branches in expression contexts. Stack (call stack) validates tag nesting |
| **AST** | JS nodes + JSX nodes (`JSXElement`, `JSXFragment`, `JSXAttribute`, `JSXExpressionContainer`, `JSXText`) |
| **CodeGen** | Walk AST, emit `React.createElement(tag, props, ...children)` |

### Key Parsing Rule
`<` starts JSX **only** in expression contexts (after `return`, `=`, `(`, in function args, etc.). In statement/operator contexts, it's the relational operator.

### Tag Matching
Recursive descent implicitly uses the **call stack** as your validation "box." Opening tag → recurse → closing tag must match. No separate stack needed unless you go iterative.

### Two Input Patterns, Same Output
```jsx
// Both parse identically — JSX only appears inside expression positions
export default function Home() { return (<Tags></Tags>); }
let Home = () => { return (<Tags></Tags>); }
```

### MVP Scope
- Opening/closing/self-closing tags
- Attributes: string literals, `{expressions}`, spread `...obj`
- Children: text, nested elements, `{expressions}`
- Fragments `<>...</>`
- Basic error messages (line/col, expected vs got)

### Time Estimate
**1–2 weeks** for working MVP. **2–3 weeks** if you want solid error diagnostics.

## Project layout

```
d:\Yggdrasil\
├── .clangd                        ← tells clangd to add -Iinclude (fixes IDE errors)
├── Makefile                       ← all/debug/release/test/clean targets
├── build.ps1                      ← PowerShell wrapper (Debug|Release|Clean|Test)
│
├── include/                       ← ALL headers
│   ├── token.h                    ← TokenKind enum + Token struct
│   ├── lexer.h                    ← Lexer class + LexMode enum
│   ├── ast.h                      ← NodeKind enum, Node struct, makeNode()
│   ├── parser.h                   ← Parser class + ParseError
│   ├── codegen.h                  ← CodeGen class
│   └── diagnostics.h              ← LexError, ParseError, CodeGenError, formatDiag()
│
├── src/                           ← ALL implementation files
│   ├── lexer.cpp
│   ├── parser.cpp
│   ├── codegen.cpp
│   └── main.cpp
│
└── tests/
    ├── fixtures/                  ← 30 positive + 2 error test cases
    │   ├── 01–30 *.jsx + *.expected.js
    │   └── err_01–02 *.jsx + *.expected_exit
    └── runner/run_tests.ps1       ← coloured diff runner with pass/fail/skip counts
```

## Build

```powershell
.\build.ps1                        # debug build → bin\yggdrasil.exe
.\build.ps1 -Mode Release
.\build.ps1 -Mode Test             # runs all fixtures
.\tests\runner\run_tests.ps1       # standalone runner with diff output
make                               # from Git Bash / WSL
make test
```

436 assistant_text: The parser is failing on `<input disabled />` — it's consuming `disabled` as the attribute name, then seeing `/` and expecting another attribute name instead of recognising `/>`. The JSX attribute loop needs to stop when it sees `/>`. Let me check the parser's attribute-scanning loop:
437 tool_call:Grep: 
439 assistant_text: The loop condition is correct. The real problem is the lexer: when in `JSXTag` mode, it sees `/` then `>` but the `/>` compound token (`JSXSlashGreater`) is only scanned if `/` is the *first* character peeked. But after emitting `disabled` as an `Identifier`, the lexer is still in `JSXTag` mode and should see `/>` — yet the parser never pushed `JSXTag` mode onto the lexer in the first place. The parser and lexer mode stacks are never connected: the parser has `inExpressionContext_` but never calls `lexer.pushMode()`. Since tokenisation is done eagerly upfront (all tokens produced before parsing starts), the mode stack on the lexer is irrelevant at parse time — but that means the lexer had no JSX context when it scanned the tokens, so it never emitted `JSXLess`, `JSXSlashGreater` etc.

The fix: the lexer's eager `tokenize()` needs to self-manage its own mode stack as it scans — driven by the tokens it sees, not by the parser. Let me rewrite the lexer to track JSX state internally:
440 tool_call:Read: d:\Yggdrasil\src\lexer.cpp
444 assistant_text: Now I see the full picture. The tokenizer uses `currentMode()` but the mode stack is only ever `[JS]` — nothing ever pushes `JSXTag` or `JSXChildren` during tokenization. The lexer needs to self-manage its mode stack as it emits tokens. Rewriting `tokenize()` to push/pop modes itself based on what it sees:
445 tool_call:Edit: d:\Yggdrasil\src\lexer.cpp

794 user_text: time for stage 2/II

MVP Stage 2 — "Solid Tool"
After your basic transpiler works on single files, Stage 2 is about making it robust, usable, and fast without turning it into Babel.
1. Error Recovery & Diagnostics

    Don't crash on first error — resynchronize and report multiple errors per file
    Track source locations in AST nodes (line/column) for precise error pointing even without source maps
    Contextual error messages — "Expected > but found } — did you forget to close an attribute?"

2. Complete JSX Spec Coverage

    Member expression tags: <My.Component />, <context.Provider />
    Namespace tags: <svg:circle /> (if you care about SVG/mathML)
    Spread children: {...items} (rare but valid)
    Whitespace normalization in JSXText — collapse rules differ from HTML
    Comments inside JSX: {/* this */} properly stripped or preserved
    Boolean attributes: <input disabled /> → disabled: true

3. Configurable Output (via config.json or cmdline args)

    Pragma switching: React.createElement vs h() (Preact) vs _jsx (React 17+ automatic runtime)
    Development vs production flags: __source and __self props for React dev warnings
    JSX runtime import injection (React 17+ style: import { jsx } from 'react/jsx-runtime')

4. Performance & Architecture

    Arena allocator for AST nodes instead of individual new/delete or heavy smart pointer churn
    Zero-copy lexer — slice strings from source buffer instead of allocating
    Streaming/batch mode — transpile entire directories, not just stdin/single file
    CLI with flags: --pragma, --out-dir, --watch

5. Edge Case Hardening

    Unicode identifiers in tag names and attributes
    Complex expressions in attributes: <div attr={foo.bar[baz]()} />
    Nested ternaries with JSX: cond ? <A /> : <B /> (parser precedence handling)
    Adjacent JSX elements in expressions: (<></>) fragments wrapping siblings

Stage 2 Time Estimate
+2–3 weeks on top of MVP.

Yggdrasil — Stage 3 Summary
Directory CLI & Batch Transpiler
What It Is
Yggdrasil shifts from single-file stdin/stdout to a directory-aware CLI tool. Point it at a source folder, get transpiled JS in a dist/ mirror. No module resolution, no bundling — still just a transpiler.
Core Features
Table
Feature	Description
Recursive directory scan	src/ → dist/ tree mirroring via std::filesystem
Batch transpilation	All .jsx files processed in parallel (thread pool)
Passthrough mode	Plain .js files copied untouched (optional --no-copy)
Watch mode	Re-transpile changed files on save (polling or OS-native watcher)
Extension rewrite	.jsx → .js in output; directory structure preserved
CLI Interface
plain
Copy

yggdrasil src/ --out-dir dist/
yggdrasil src/ --out-dir dist/ --watch
yggdrasil src/ --out-dir dist/ --jsx-pragma h --no-copy --verbose

Table
Flag	Behavior
--out-dir <path>	Required for batch mode
--watch	Monitor source and re-transpile deltas
--jsx-pragma <name>	Override React.createElement
--jsx-runtime <classic|automatic>	React 17+ _jsx runtime vs classic
--no-copy	Skip non-JSX .js files
--verbose	Print src/components/Button.jsx → dist/components/Button.js
What Yggdrasil Stage 3 Does NOT Do

    ❌ Parse or resolve import/export
    ❌ Touch node_modules
    ❌ Build dependency graphs
    ❌ Bundle, concatenate, or tree-shake
    ❌ Handle CSS, assets, or non-JS files beyond copy/passthrough
    ❌ Source maps (already excluded by design)

C++ Implementation Notes

    std::filesystem::recursive_directory_iterator for scanning
    Thread pool (std::thread + std::queue + mutex) for parallel file processing
    Watch mode: simple polling loop (cross-platform) or OS-specific APIs (inotify/FSEvents/ReadDirectoryChangesW) for efficiency
    Error isolation: one file failing doesn't kill the batch; aggregate error report at end

Output Example
plain
Copy

src/
  components/
    Button.jsx      →  dist/components/Button.js
    Icon.jsx        →  dist/components/Icon.js
  pages/
    Home.jsx        →  dist/pages/Home.js
  utils/
    helpers.js      →  dist/utils/helpers.js   (copied)

```cpp
if (cp->cand_count > 1U) {
    cs.top++;  /* push choice point for remaining candidates */
}

if (!try_clause(first_pos, dgoal, &gs, env)) {
    if (cp->cand_count > 1U) {
        /* choice point was pushed — backtrack into it */
        goto backtrack;
    }
    goto backtrack;
}
```

When `cand_count = 1` and `try_clause` succeeds: goal stack gets `path(fresh_X, fresh_Y)` with `fresh_X=a`, `fresh_Y=c` bound. Good.

Now resolve `path(a, c)`:
- `path/2` has 3 clauses: `path(a,b)`, `path(b,c)`, `path(a,c)`  
- First-arg index: bucket `a` → [path(a,b), path(a,c)]; bucket `b` → [path(b,c)]
- Query: `path(a, c)` → ground `a` → returns bucket `a` = 2 candidates
- `cand_count = 2` → push choice point, try first candidate `path(a,b)`
- Unify `path(a,c)` with `path(a,b)`: `b ≠ c` → FAIL
- Backtrack to choice point for `path`, try `path(a,c)`  
- Unify `path(a,c)` with `path(a,c)` → SUCCESS
- Goal stack empty → solution found!

This should work. Let me add a debug log to see what's happening. Actually let me look at the `try_clause` function more carefully — the `trail_mark` issue:

In `try_clause`:
```cpp
static RenameMap rm;
memset(&rm, 0, sizeof(RenameMap));
const uint32_t mark = trail_mark(&env->trail);
```

`mark` is the trail state **before** the rename+unify attempt. If unify fails, we call `trail_undo(&env->trail, env->vars, mark)` which undoes back to pre-rename state. Good.

But in the backtrack loop:
```cpp
trail_undo(&env->trail, env->vars, cp->trail_mark);
```

`cp->trail_mark` is the trail state when the choice point was created. When we first enter backtrack for a choice point that was just pushed (for `path(a,b)`/`path(a,c)`) — the trail_mark was set before the FIRST candidate was tried. But `try_clause` for the first candidate already undid to its own mark on failure. So `cp->trail_mark` and the current trail state are both at the pre-candidate position. `trail_undo` to `cp->trail_mark` is a no-op.

Wait — looking at the forward section again:
```cpp
cp->trail_mark = trail_mark(&env->trail);
cp->goal_top   = gs.top;
...
const uint32_t first_pos = cp->candidates[0];
cp->cand_idx = 1U;

if (cp->cand_count > 1U) {
    cs.top++;  /* push choice point */
}

if (!try_clause(first_pos, dgoal, &gs, env)) {
    goto backtrack;
}
```

The `trail_mark` is taken BEFORE `try_clause`. `try_clause` creates its own internal mark. If `try_clause` fails, it calls `trail_undo(env->trail, env->vars, mark)` where `mark` = trail state at entry to `try_clause`. So after a failed `try_clause`, the trail is at the same state as when `cp->trail_mark` was set. 

Now in the forward section when `try_clause` **succeeds** for `reachable/2`:
- Trail advances (binding `fresh_X=a`, `fresh_Y=c`)
- Body goal `path(fresh_X, fresh_Y)` = `path(a, c)` pushed
- Forward to next iteration
- Pop `path(a,c)`, look up `path/2`, get candidates [path(a,b), path(a,c)]
- `cp2->trail_mark = trail_mark(trail)` ← this is AFTER the `reachable` bindings
- Try `path(a,b)` → fails → trail undone to `cp2->trail_mark`
- Backtrack: `trail_undo(env->trail, vars, cp2->trail_mark)` ← same state, no-op
- Try `path(a,c)` → succeeds!
- `cs.top` decremented (it was the last candidate)
- `goto next_iter` → continue

That should work! But the test reports 0 solutions. Let me look at what happens specifically with the static `SolverCtx` members. The previous test is:

```
TEST("multiple clauses — correct solution count")
{
    SolverCtx ctx;
    ctx.load("fruit(apple). fruit(banana). fruit(cherry).");
    EXPECT_EQ(ctx.query("?- fruit(X)."), 3U);
}
```

And the failing test:
```
TEST("backtracking finds second solution after first fails")
{
    SolverCtx ctx;
    ctx.load("path(a, b). path(b, c). path(a, c). "
             "reachable(X, Y) :- path(X, Y).");
    EXPECT_EQ(ctx.query("?- reachable(a, c)."), 1U);
}
```

Since `SolverCtx` reuses static members, when `ctx.query(...)` is called, `query_arena` is re-inited but `clause_arena` is NOT re-inited (only `clause_vars` is re-inited). The terms for `path(a,b)` etc. are stored in `clause_arena`. The `db` accumulates all clauses from all tests.

But `reachable/2` should have exactly 1 clause from this test load, and `path/2` should have exactly 3 clauses. Since no previous test defined these predicates, the counts should be right.

Let me add a targeted debug print to understand what's happening — actually, let me check if this is a static-scope issue with the query result callback. Looking at `test_solver.cpp` line 85-88:

```cpp
return solve(&env, pq.goals, pq.goal_count,
             [](SolverEnv *, const TermRef *, uint32_t, void *) {
                 return SOLVE_NEXT;
             }, nullptr);
```

The `solve` function returns the number of solutions. If it returns 0 for `reachable(a, c)`, it means either the goal failed or the index is returning wrong candidates.

Let me check if the issue might be in the static `clause_vars` being reset between clause loads but the variables from previous loads still being in the static `clause_arena`. The `rename_term` function copies from `clause_arena` into `query_arena`. If `clause_vars` was reset but `clause_arena` contains variables from old clause loads...

Actually `clause_vars` is re-init'd in `load()`: `var_registry_init(&clause_vars)`. This clears all variable registrations. But the term nodes in `clause_arena` still reference old `VarId`s. After reset, `var_alloc` starts from index 1 again, potentially creating a new variable with the same `VarId` as an old one.

This is the key bug: `var_registry_init` resets the count to 0, so the next `var_alloc` returns `VarId=1`. But a previously stored term in `clause_arena` also references `VarId=1` (from a previous load). When `rename_term` copies that term and calls `var_alloc` on `env->vars`, it creates a fresh var with ID `n` in the query's `VarRegistry`, which is correct. But the issue is that **two different logical variables from different clauses share the same `VarId` in clause_vars**, so when renamed into the query arena, they might collide in the `RenameMap`.

Wait — `rename_term` creates new `VarId`s in `env->vars` (the query vars), not in `clause_vars`. And it uses the `RenameMap` to ensure that within a single clause rename, the same old `VarId` maps to the same fresh `VarId`. But the old `VarId`s in the **same** clause are all unique (since they were allocated sequentially within `clause_vars` before it was reset). The problem would only arise if two clauses were renamed simultaneously sharing the same `RenameMap`, but that doesn't happen.

The real issue is that after `var_registry_init(&clause_vars)`, the VarId counter resets to 0. The next load will allocate `VarId=1, 2, 3...` again. Two clauses loaded in different `load()` calls with an intervening `var_registry_init` will have their variables with overlapping `VarId`s (both use 1, 2, 3...). When `rename_term` processes clause 1 (VarId=1 → fresh1) and then clause 2 (VarId=1 → fresh2?), the `RenameMap` is cleared per clause, so no collision.

Let me approach this differently — just add a minimal debug trace and run:

int main() {
    static AtomTable   atoms;
    static Database    db;
    static TermArena   clause_arena;
    static VarRegistry clause_vars;
    static TermArena   query_arena;
    static VarRegistry query_vars;

    atom_table_init(&atoms);
    db_init(&db);
    term_arena_init(&clause_arena);
    var_registry_init(&clause_vars);

    auto load = [&](const char *src) {
        static TokenList toks;
        memset(&toks, 0, sizeof(toks));
        if (lex(src, &toks) < 0) { printf("LEX FAIL\n"); return; }
        var_registry_init(&clause_vars);
        Parser p;
        if (parser_init(&p, &toks, &clause_arena, &atoms, &clause_vars) < 0) { printf("PARSER INIT FAIL\n"); return; }
        ParsedClause c;
        for (;;) {
            memset(&c, 0, sizeof(c));
            ParseResult r = parser_next_clause(&p, &c);
            if (r == PARSE_EOF) break;
            if (r != PARSE_OK) { printf("PARSE FAIL %d\n", r); return; }
            if (db_assert_last(&db, &c, &clause_arena) < 0) { printf("ASSERT FAIL\n"); return; }
        }
    };

    auto run_query = [&](const char *src) -> uint32_t {
        static TokenList toks;
        memset(&toks, 0, sizeof(toks));
        term_arena_init(&query_arena);
        var_registry_init(&query_vars);
        static char buf[256];
        snprintf(buf, sizeof(buf), "?- %s.", src);
        if (lex(buf, &toks) < 0) return 0;
        Parser p;
        if (parser_init(&p, &toks, &query_arena, &atoms, &query_vars) < 0) return 0;
        ParsedQuery pq;
        if (parser_query(&p, &pq) != PARSE_OK) return 0;
        SolverEnv env;
        solver_env_init(&env, &query_arena, &clause_arena, &query_vars, &clause_vars, &atoms, &db);
        return solve(&env, pq.goals, pq.goal_count,
                     [](SolverEnv*, const TermRef*, uint32_t, void*) { return SOLVE_NEXT; }, nullptr);
    };

    load("path(a, b). path(b, c). path(a, c). reachable(X, Y) :- path(X, Y).");

    AtomId path_id = atom_lookup(&atoms, "path");
    AtomId reach_id = atom_lookup(&atoms, "reachable");
    printf("path/2 pred: %p\n", (void*)db_get_pred(&db, path_id, 2));
    printf("reachable/2 pred: %p\n", (void*)db_get_pred(&db, reach_id, 2));
    const PredEntry *pp = db_get_pred(&db, path_id, 2);
    const PredEntry *rp = db_get_pred(&db, reach_id, 2);

                   pp->count, (int)pp->index.built, pp->index.idx_bucket_count, pp->index.idx_var_count);
    if (rp) printf("reach count=%u index_built=%d buckets=%u var_clauses=%u\n",
                   rp->count, (int)rp->index.built, rp->index.idx_bucket_count, rp->index.idx_var_count);

    uint32_t n = run_query("reachable(a, c)");
    printf("Solutions: %u (expected 1)\n", n);
    return n == 1 ? 0 : 1;
}

$src = @'
#include "../include/solver.hpp"
#include "../include/builtins.hpp"
#include "../include/parser.hpp"
#include "../include/lexer.hpp"
#include <cstdio>
#include <cstring>

// Patched solve that prints each step
static AtomTable   g_atoms;

const char *atom_name(AtomId id) {
    return atom_spelling(&g_atoms, id);
}

void print_term(TermRef ref, const TermArena *arena, const VarRegistry *vars, int depth = 0) {
    if (depth > 5 || ref == TERM_REF_INVALID) { printf("?"); return; }
    TermRef dr = deref(ref, arena, vars);
    const Term *t = term_get(arena, dr);
    if (!t) { printf("NULL"); return; }
    switch (t->kind) {
        case TERM_KIND_ATOM: printf("%s", atom_name(t->data.atom_id)); break;
        case TERM_KIND_INT:  printf("%d", t->data.int_val); break;
        case TERM_KIND_VAR:  printf("_%u", t->data.var.var_id); break;
        case TERM_KIND_COMPOUND:
            printf("%s(", atom_name(t->data.compound.functor));
            for (uint8_t i = 0; i < t->data.compound.arity; i++) {
                if (i) printf(",");
                print_term(term_arg(arena, dr, i), arena, vars, depth+1);
            }
            printf(")");
            break;
        default: printf("?kind");
    }
}

int main() {
    static Database    db;
    static TermArena   clause_arena;
    static VarRegistry clause_vars;
    static TermArena   query_arena;
    static VarRegistry query_vars;

    atom_table_init(&g_atoms);
    db_init(&db);
    term_arena_init(&clause_arena);
    var_registry_init(&clause_vars);

    const char *prog = "path(a, b). path(b, c). path(a, c). reachable(X, Y) :- path(X, Y).";
    static TokenList toks;
    memset(&toks, 0, sizeof(toks));
    lex(prog, &toks);
    var_registry_init(&clause_vars);
    Parser p;
    parser_init(&p, &toks, &clause_arena, &g_atoms, &clause_vars);
    ParsedClause c;
    for (;;) {
        memset(&c, 0, sizeof(c));
        ParseResult r = parser_next_clause(&p, &c);
        if (r == PARSE_EOF) break;
        if (r != PARSE_OK) { printf("PARSE ERR\n"); break; }
        db_assert_last(&db, &c, &clause_arena);
    }

    // Print path/2 index
    AtomId path_id = atom_lookup(&g_atoms, "path");
    const PredEntry *pp = db_get_pred(&db, path_id, 2);
    printf("path/2: count=%u buckets=%u\n", pp->count, pp->index.idx_bucket_count);
    for (uint32_t bi = 0; bi < pp->index.idx_bucket_count; bi++) {
        printf("  bucket[%u]: kind=%u start=%u count=%u\n", bi,
               pp->index.idx_kind[bi], pp->index.idx_start[bi], pp->index.idx_count[bi]);
        for (uint32_t si = 0; si < pp->index.idx_count[bi]; si++) {
            uint32_t pos = pp->index.idx_clause_slots[pp->index.idx_start[bi] + si];
            const StoredClause *sc = db_get_clause(&db, pos);
            printf("    slot[%u]=pos%u head=", si, pos);
            print_term(sc->head, &clause_arena, &clause_vars);
            printf("\n");
        }
    }

    // Query
    term_arena_init(&query_arena);
    var_registry_init(&query_vars);
    static TokenList toks2;
    memset(&toks2, 0, sizeof(toks2));
    lex("?- reachable(a, c).", &toks2);
    Parser p2;
    parser_init(&p2, &toks2, &query_arena, &g_atoms, &query_vars);
    ParsedQuery pq;
    parser_query(&p2, &pq);

    uint32_t call_n = 0;
    struct Dbg { TermArena *qa; VarRegistry *qv; uint32_t *n; };
    Dbg dbg = {&query_arena, &query_vars, &call_n};

    SolverEnv env;
    solver_env_init(&env, &query_arena, &clause_arena, &query_vars, &clause_vars, &g_atoms, &db);
    uint32_t n = solve(&env, pq.goals, pq.goal_count,
         [](SolverEnv *e, const TermRef *gs, uint32_t gc, void *ud) {
             Dbg *d = (Dbg*)ud;
             (*d->n)++;
             printf("SOLUTION %u: ", *d->n);
             for (uint32_t i = 0; i < gc; i++) {
                 print_term(gs[i], e->arena, e->vars);
                 printf(" ");
             }
             printf("\n");
             return SOLVE_NEXT;
         }, &dbg);
    printf("Total solutions: %u\n", n);
    return n == 1 ? 0 : 1;
}

- `idx_kind[64]` = 64 bytes  
- `idx_start[64]` + `idx_count[64]` = 512 bytes
- `idx_clause_slots[256]` = 1KB
- `idx_slot_count` = 4 bytes
- `idx_var_slots[64]` = 256 bytes
- `idx_var_count` + `built` = ~8 bytes
- Total FirstArgIndex ≈ **2.1KB**

`PredEntry` = functor + arity + first + count + `FirstArgIndex` ≈ **2.1KB**

`Database` = `StoredClause clauses[4096]` + `PredEntry preds[512]` 

`StoredClause` size: `head(4) + body[32×4=128] + body_len(4) + id(4)` = **136 bytes**

`Database` = `4096 × 136 + 512 × ~2100` ≈ `557KB + 1075KB` ≈ **1.6MB**

And `IdxCtx` has TWO `TermArena`s. Each `TermArena` = 8192 × `sizeof(Term)` + `ArgPool`.

`Term` size ≈ 4 (kind) + max(4,4,12,4) (union) + 4 (ref) + padding = ~24 bytes.
`TermArena` = 8192 × 24 + 4 + 32768 × 4 + 4 ≈ **328KB**.

`IdxCtx` total ≈ `AtomTable(1MB) + Database(1.6MB) + 2 × TermArena(328KB each) + 2 × VarRegistry(1024 × ~80bytes = 82KB each)` ≈ **3.4MB**.

With `IdxCtx` as a local variable on the stack in each test case, and ~12 test cases, the test framework's `run_all()` calls them sequentially (no nesting), so only one is on the stack at a time. But the 3.4MB still exceeds the default Windows stack size (1MB).

The fix: make `IdxCtx` members static in the test, similar to how `test_solver.cpp` does it:
263 tool_call:Read: d:\Epilog\tests\test_indexing.cpp
267 assistant_text: The `IdxCtx` struct members need to become static like in `test_solver.cpp`. Let me rewrite `test_indexing.cpp` to use static members:

FrostCore Stage 1 — "Allocator & Lifecycle Foundation"
Since quickjs-ng is a living C codebase, Stage 1 isn't rewriting — it's establishing the extraction boundary. You fork, you build, you inject control points.
Core Tasks
Table
#	Task	What It Means
1	Fork & Branch	Mirror quickjs-ng as upstream branch. All work happens on frostcore branch. Document rebase strategy for pulling upstream fixes.
2	Build System Migration	Replace whatever build quickjs-ng uses (likely meson or their own make) with your Makefile + build.sh/build.ps1. Must produce libfrostcore.a and frostcore.h cleanly.
3	Allocator Injection	Audit every malloc/free/realloc in the engine. Route them through function pointers stored in JSRuntime (or your wrapper struct). GlacioJS must be able to pass: alloc_fn, free_fn, realloc_fn, plus an opaque userdata pointer.
4	Explicit Lifecycle API	C wrapper around runtime creation/destruction: frost_runtime_create(), frost_runtime_destroy(). No hidden globals. Multiple runtimes must coexist without cross-contamination.
5	Minimal Eval Hook	frost_eval(runtime, source) — just enough JS execution surface for GlacioJS to test the handoff. No stdlib modules yet, just raw expression evaluation.
6	Smoke Test	Create 3+ runtimes simultaneously with different allocator configs, eval something, destroy them. Prove isolation works.
Deliverables
plain
Copy

libfrostcore.a          // Static library
include/frostcore.h     // Public C API (GlacioJS includes this)
Makefile
build.sh / build.ps1

The C API Surface (Stage 1)
c
Copy

typedef struct frost_alloc {
    void* (*alloc)(size_t size, void* userdata);
    void  (*free)(void* ptr, void* userdata);
    void* (*realloc)(void* ptr, size_t size, void* userdata);
    void* userdata;  // per-isolate arena, tracing hooks, etc.
} frost_alloc;

typedef struct frost_runtime frost_runtime;

frost_runtime* frost_runtime_create(const frost_alloc* alloc);
void           frost_runtime_destroy(frost_runtime* rt);

// Minimal eval for integration testing
int frost_eval(frost_runtime* rt, const char* source, char** out_error);

Explicitly NOT in Stage 1

    ❌ Parser/bytecode modularity (still one compilation unit)
    ❌ GC algorithm changes (keep quickjs-ng's ref-count + cycle GC)
    ❌ Stdlib separation (console, setTimeout, etc. still baked in or disabled, not modularized)
    ❌ C++ wrapper (pure C boundary)
    ❌ Task/isolate scheduler (that's GlacioJS Layer 2 using FrostCore runtimes)
    ❌ ES module loader hooks

214 user_text: PLAN and DO STAGE 2, ALSO ADD TEST CASES TO IT

FrostCore Stage 2 — "Subsystem Split"
After allocator injection, the next cut is separating the engine from the stdlib. quickjs-ng bundles console, setTimeout, basic I/O, and utility objects into the runtime init. Stage 2 makes them optional.
Core Tasks
Table
#	Task	What It Means
1	Audit stdlib coupling	Find every JS_AddIntrinsic*() call and js_init_*() function in quickjs-ng's runtime init. Map what depends on what.
2	Extract core-only runtime	Create frost_runtime_create_bare() — a runtime with zero JS stdlib. No console, no Date, no JSON, no setTimeout. Just the VM, GC, and parser.
3	Subsystem headers	Group stdlib functions by domain: frost_std_core.h (Object, Array, JSON), frost_std_io.h (console, print), frost_std_time.h (Date, timers).
4	Registration API	frost_register_std_core(rt), frost_register_std_io(rt), etc. GlacioJS calls these explicitly after frost_runtime_create().
5	Stub unregistered calls	If console isn't registered, console.log throws a clean ReferenceError instead of segfaulting or silently failing.
6	Size audit	Measure binary size difference between full stdlib and bare runtime. Prove modularity has tangible impact.
Deliverables
plain
Copy

libfrostcore.a              // Still one library, but internally split
libfrostcore_bare.a         // (Optional) VM only, for extreme embedding
include/frostcore.h         // Updated: registration functions added
include/frostcore_subsystems.h  // Optional subsystem registry

The Registration API
c
Copy

// After Stage 1: bare runtime
frost_runtime* rt = frost_runtime_create(&alloc);

// Stage 2: GlacioJS chooses what to inject
frost_register_std_core(rt);    // Object, Array, Function, JSON, Math
frost_register_std_io(rt);      // console, print (if any)
frost_register_std_time(rt);    // Date, timers (if quickjs-ng has them)

// GlacioJS native modules come later (Layer 3)
// frost_register_http(rt);
// frost_register_fs(rt);

Explicitly NOT in Stage 2

    ❌ Parser/bytecode modularity (still one compilation unit)
    ❌ GC replacement or algorithm changes
    ❌ Isolate/task scheduler (GlacioJS Layer 2)
    ❌ ES module loader hooks
    ❌ C++ wrapper or embedding API beyond C

Why This Order
Allocator first (Stage 1), then stdlib (Stage 2) because:

    You can't measure per-subsystem memory cost until allocations are tracked
    GlacioJS's philosophy demands "optional subsystem registration" — this makes it real
    It proves the runtime can start minimal and grow capabilities selectively

335 tool_call:Grep: 
337 tool_call:Read: /d/FrostCore/quickjs.c
339 assistant_text: `ctx->eval_internal` is set to `NULL` by default and `JS_AddIntrinsicEval` sets it to `__JS_EvalInternal`. The C-level `JS_Eval()` call hits the `eval is not supported` check when `eval_internal` is NULL — even for `frost_eval()`. So on a bare context, **no JS can be evaluated at all** until `JS_AddIntrinsicEval` is called.

The fix: `frost_runtime_create_bare()` must call `JS_AddIntrinsicEval` (just the eval_internal pointer assignment, zero JS objects) so the VM is usable. This does NOT add `eval()` to the global scope — that's done by `JS_AddIntrinsicBaseObjects` which adds the `eval` function. `JS_AddIntrinsicEval` only enables the C-level execution path.

Also fix T2: the test strings `"std_core"` need to match the actual error messages which say `"requires std_core"` — this DOES match since `strstr("requires std_core", "std_core")` is true. The failures are purely stderr/stdout ordering. I'll separate them into different streams to confirm, then the real fix is just T1.

---

### API summary

```c
frost_runtime *rt = frost_runtime_create_bare(&alloc);  // zero stdlib

frost_register_std_core(rt, NULL);        // Object, Array, Math, JSON, RegExp, Map…
frost_register_std_collections(rt, NULL); // TypedArrays, WeakRef
frost_register_std_async(rt, NULL);       // Promise, atob/btoa
frost_register_std_time(rt, NULL);        // Date, performance
frost_register_std_io(rt, NULL);          // console.log, print()
// — or —
frost_register_std_all(rt, NULL);         // all of the above

frost_subsystems_registered(rt);          // → FROST_STD_CORE | FROST_STD_ASYNC | …
```

### Non-obvious fix documented

`frost_runtime_create_bare()` must call `JS_AddIntrinsicEval()` even though it installs nothing visible in JS. Without it, `ctx->eval_internal` stays NULL and every `frost_eval()` call — even `"1+2"` — throws `"eval is not supported"` at the C level. `JS_AddIntrinsicEval` sets the function pointer; `JS_AddIntrinsicBaseObjects` (inside `frost_register_std_core`) is what puts `eval` into the JS global scope.

### Size audit

```
frostcore.o (boundary layer):   1.5 KB text+data
frostcore_subsystems.o:         3.8 KB
quickjs.o (engine):           954   KB  ← linker will strip what GlacioJS doesn't call
quickjs-libc.o (io):           46   KB
libfrostcore.a total:           1.9 MB (unstripped archive)
```

### API summary

```c
frost_runtime *rt = frost_runtime_create_bare(&alloc);  // zero stdlib

frost_register_std_core(rt, NULL);        // Object, Array, Math, JSON, RegExp, Map…
frost_register_std_collections(rt, NULL); // TypedArrays, WeakRef
frost_register_std_async(rt, NULL);       // Promise, atob/btoa
frost_register_std_time(rt, NULL);        // Date, performance
frost_register_std_io(rt, NULL);          // console.log, print()
// — or —
frost_register_std_all(rt, NULL);         // all of the above

frost_subsystems_registered(rt);          // → FROST_STD_CORE | FROST_STD_ASYNC | …
```

### Non-obvious fix documented

`frost_runtime_create_bare()` must call `JS_AddIntrinsicEval()` even though it installs nothing visible in JS. Without it, `ctx->eval_internal` stays NULL and every `frost_eval()` call — even `"1+2"` — throws `"eval is not supported"` at the C level. `JS_AddIntrinsicEval` sets the function pointer; `JS_AddIntrinsicBaseObjects` (inside `frost_register_std_core`) is what puts `eval` into the JS global scope.

```
frostcore.o (boundary layer):   1.5 KB text+data

quickjs.o (engine):           954   KB  ← linker will strip what GlacioJS doesn't call
quickjs-libc.o (io):           46   KB
libfrostcore.a total:           1.9 MB (unstripped archive)
```

433 user_text: Time for Stage 3,
FrostCore Stage 3 — "Parser/Bytecode Boundary"
After memory and stdlib are modular, the next cut is separating the frontend from the backend. quickjs-ng's parser emits bytecode directly into runtime-specific structures. Stage 3 makes the parser a standalone compilation unit that produces a portable intermediate representation the VM can load independently.
Core Tasks
Table
#	Task	What It Means
1	Extract parser state	Remove parser's hidden dependency on JSRuntime internals. Parser should only need: source string, allocator, error callback.
2	Bytecode container	Define frost_bytecode struct — a self-contained blob of opcodes, constants, atom table, and function tables. No runtime pointers.
3	Parse interface	frost_parse(const char* source, frost_bytecode** out, frost_error* err) — source in, bytecode out.
4	Load interface	frost_runtime_load(rt, frost_bytecode* bc) — VM ingests pre-parsed bytecode. Enables parse-once, run-many.
5	Atom table isolation	Parser builds its own atom table during parse. Runtime merges it on load. Breaks the current global atom table assumption.
6	Compilation unit split	Physical separation: frost_parse.c (lexer + parser), frost_bytecode.c (IR format), frost_vm.c (execution engine).
Deliverables
plain
Copy
src/
  frost_alloc.c       // Stage 1
  frost_runtime.c     // Stage 1
  frost_std_*.c       // Stage 2
  frost_parse.c       // Stage 3 (NEW)
  frost_bytecode.c    // Stage 3 (NEW)
  frost_vm.c          // Stage 3 (split from monolith)
include/
  frostcore.h         // Updated with parse/load API
  frost_bytecode.h    // Bytecode IR definition (opaque to GlacioJS)
The API Surface (Stage 3)
c
Copy
// Opaque handle to compiled bytecode
typedef struct frost_bytecode frost_bytecode;

// Parse source into portable bytecode (no runtime needed)
int frost_parse(const char* source, frost_bytecode** out, frost_error* err);

// Load bytecode into a specific runtime for execution
int frost_runtime_load(frost_runtime* rt, frost_bytecode* bc);

// Execute already-loaded bytecode
int frost_runtime_eval_loaded(frost_runtime* rt, frost_bytecode* bc, JSValue* out);

// Destroy bytecode independently of runtime lifecycle
void frost_bytecode_destroy(frost_bytecode* bc);
Explicitly NOT in Stage 3
❌ C++ wrapper or AST exposure (bytecode is the boundary, not AST nodes)
❌ JIT compilation or codegen changes
❌ ES module resolution or loader hooks
❌ GC algorithm changes
❌ Multi-threaded parsing (single-threaded only)
❌ Isolate/task scheduler (still GlacioJS Layer 2)
Why This Order
Allocator → Stdlib → Parser/VM split because:
GlacioJS can now parse JSX (via Yggdrasil) or JS once, cache the bytecode, and inject it into multiple FrostCore runtimes/isolates
The VM becomes smaller — it doesn't need parser tables or lexer state if only running pre-compiled code
It proves the runtime is a true engine that consumes fuel (bytecode), not a monolithic compiler-interpreter
Time Estimate
2–3 weeks
The hard part is decoupling the atom table. quickjs-ng uses a global/runtime-wide atom table for string deduplication and property keys. Making the parser build a local one that gets merged on load requires careful refcount handling.
436 assistant_text: Let me audit the actual quickjs-ng internals before writing a single line — Stage 3 depends entirely on what's actually entangled.
437 tool_call:Agent: 
444 assistant_text: The audit result changes Stage 3 fundamentally. Here's what it reveals:

**`JS_WriteObject` / `JS_ReadObject` already exist and already do atom remapping.** They produce a self-contained binary blob with no runtime pointers. The parser cannot be physically separated from the VM without a massive rewrite (codegen is interleaved with parsing, 63K line monolith). Stage 3 in the spec assumes a separation that doesn't exist in quickjs-ng's architecture.

**What Stage 3 actually is, given reality:** Build the `frost_parse` / `frost_runtime_load` / `frost_bytecode_destroy` API as a clean boundary over `JS_WriteObject` / `JS_ReadObject`. The serialization format is the portable IR. Parse-once-run-many works today via this path. The "compilation unit split" from the spec is deferred — that requires rewriting quickjs-ng itself, not wrapping it.

The `_draw_world_setup` returns `true` when Generate is clicked — but the original never used that. So the transition to Residents was **never through World_Setup returning true**. The original flow must be: generate the world inline when Back is pressed from Residents, or Residents is reached differently. 

But wait — actually the problem is simpler. The original `case .World_Setup` just called `_draw_world_setup(app)` and ignored the bool. The screen never advanced from World_Setup to Residents automatically — it relied on the user pressing some other button. But there's no such button in `_draw_world_setup`. 

This means the original had a bug — or more likely, the island was pre-seeded somewhere else. Looking at `main()`: there's no `starters_seed` call at init. So the original flow **was** broken, and the intent was that `_draw_world_setup` returning `true` should advance to Residents. My `if` fix is correct — but it's now calling `starters_seed` every time Generate is clicked, which is right.

The "no screen/output" issue is something else entirely. Let me check if the process is actually launching visibly:

# Material Design 3

This skill guides implementation of Google's Material Design 3 (MD3) — a personal, adaptive, expressive design system. MD3 uses dynamic color, tonal surfaces, rounded shapes, and spring-based motion to create UIs that feel alive and personal.

## Philosophy

MD3 is built on three principles:
- **Personal**: Dynamic color adapts UI to the user's wallpaper or content. Theming is individual, not one-size-fits-all.
- **Adaptive**: Layouts transform across 5 window size classes. Components resize, reposition, and change form factor responsively.
- **Expressive**: Shape morphing, spring physics, and emphasized typography create moments of delight without sacrificing usability.

## Current Updates: Google I/O 2026

Material's [Google I/O 2026 update](https://m3.material.io/blog/whats-new-at-io26) reinforces a **Compose-first** Android path and expands expressive/adaptive guidance:

- **Material Android is Compose-first**: For new Android work, prefer Jetpack Compose Material3 for the latest components, expressive APIs, adaptive scaffolds, and Styles API integration. Android Views may remain necessary in existing apps, but they should not be treated as the default path for new Material 3 implementations.
- **Expressive layout system**: Use an expressive layout scaffold to adapt screens across mobile, desktop, foldables, watches, XR, and other spatial form factors. Start from adaptive scaffolds/window size classes instead of fixed phone-first layouts.
- **8dp spacing system**: Apply spacing tokens for margins, padding, and gaps so layouts and components can adapt programmatically to device type and density.
- **New/updated expressive components**: Lists, menus, search, and search app bars have refreshed expressive guidance, with Jetpack Compose as the primary implementation target.
- **Watches and XR**: Watches emphasize physics-based motion, arc text, and edge-hugging containers. XR emphasizes spatial panels and depth-based elevation.

**Key differences from MD2:**
- Tonal surfaces replace elevation shadows as the primary depth cue
- Dynamic color generates full schemes from a single seed color
- Fully rounded corners by default (not slightly rounded)
- Spring-based motion physics replace fixed easing curves for components
- 3 levels of user-controlled contrast (standard/medium/high)

**Relationship with frontend-design skill:**
When both skills are active, MD3 provides the design system (tokens, components, layout rules) and frontend-design provides creative direction within those constraints. MD3 rules take precedence for component structure and token usage. Note: Roboto/Roboto Flex IS the correct default typeface in MD3 — the frontend-design guidance to avoid Roboto does not apply when implementing MD3.

## Decision Tree

**What are you building?**
```
Full app scaffold        → See "Common Patterns: App Shell" + references/layout-and-responsive.md
Single component         → See "Component Quick Reference" table → references/component-catalog.md
Custom theme             → See references/theming-and-dynamic-color.md
Form / input layout      → See references/component-catalog.md § Input Components
Navigation structure     → See references/navigation-patterns.md
Data display             → See references/component-catalog.md § Data Display
```

**What platform?**
```
Jetpack Compose          → Primary: androidx.compose.material3, MaterialTheme, references/*
Flutter                  → useMaterial3: true in ThemeData, ColorScheme.fromSeed()
Web (vanilla JS)         → @material/web (limited; maintenance mode) + CSS custom properties
Web (React/Vue/Svelte)   → CSS custom properties + wrapper components (no official React lib)
Web (CSS-only)           → MD3 token values as CSS custom properties (no <md-*> elements)
```

## Design Token System

All MD3 tokens use the `md.sys` namespace. **Jetpack Compose** maps roles to `MaterialTheme.colorScheme`, `MaterialTheme.typography`, and `MaterialTheme.shapes` (same semantic roles as the spec). **On the web**, these map to CSS custom properties (`--md-sys-*`):

### Color Tokens (`--md-sys-color-*`)
| Token | Purpose |
|-------|---------|
| `primary` | High-emphasis fills, text, icons against surface |
| `on-primary` | Text/icons on primary |
| `primary-container` | Standout fill for key components (FAB, etc.) |
| `on-primary-container` | Text/icons on primary-container |
| `secondary` / `on-secondary` | Less prominent accents |
| `secondary-container` / `on-secondary-container` | Recessive components (tonal buttons) |
| `tertiary` / `on-tertiary` | Contrasting accents |
| `tertiary-container` / `on-tertiary-container` | Complementary containers |
| `error` / `on-error` | Error states (static — doesn't change with dynamic color) |
| `error-container` / `on-error-container` | Error container fills |
| `surface` | Default background |
| `on-surface` | Text/icons on any surface |
| `on-surface-variant` | Lower-emphasis text/icons on surface |
| `surface-container-lowest` | Lowest-emphasis container |
| `surface-container-low` | Low-emphasis container |
| `surface-container` | Default container (nav areas) |
| `surface-container-high` | High-emphasis container |
| `surface-container-highest` | Highest-emphasis container |
| `surface-dim` / `surface-bright` | Maintain relative brightness across light/dark |
| `inverse-surface` / `inverse-on-surface` / `inverse-primary` | Contrasting elements (snackbars) |
| `outline` | Important boundaries (text field borders) |
| `outline-variant` | Decorative elements (dividers) |

Full details: `references/color-system.md`

### Typography Tokens (`--md-sys-typescale-*`)
| Scale | Sizes | Use |
|-------|-------|-----|
| Display | L / M / S | Hero text, large numbers |
| Headline | L / M / S | Section headers |
| Title | L / M / S | Smaller headers, card titles |
| Body | L / M / S | Paragraph text, descriptions |
| Label | L / M / S | Buttons, chips, captions |

Each style has tokens for: `-font`, `-weight`, `-size`, `-line-height`, `-tracking`
Plus 15 **emphasized** variants (higher weight) via `--md-sys-typescale-emphasized-*`

Full details: `references/typography-and-shape.md`

### Shape Tokens (`--md-sys-shape-corner-*`)
| Token | Value | Example components |
|-------|-------|-------------------|
| `none` | 0dp | — |
| `extra-small` | 4dp | Chips, snackbars |
| `small` | 8dp | Text fields, menus |
| `medium` | 12dp | Cards |
| `large` | 16dp | FABs, navigation drawer |
| `large-increased` | 20dp | (Expressive) |
| `extra-large` | 28dp | Dialogs, bottom sheets |
| `extra-large-increased` | 32dp | (Expressive) |
| `extra-extra-large` | 48dp | (Expressive) |
| `full` | 9999px | Buttons, chips, badges |

### Elevation Levels
| Level | DP | Tonal offset | Use |
|-------|-----|-------------|-----|
| 0 | 0dp | None | Flat surfaces, most components at rest |
| 1 | 1dp | +5% primary | Elevated cards, modal sheets |
| 2 | 3dp | +8% primary | Menus, nav bar, scrolled app bar |
| 3 | 6dp | +11% primary | FAB, dialogs, search, date/time pickers |
| 4 | 8dp | +12% primary | (hover/focus increase only) |
| 5 | 12dp | +14% primary | (hover/focus increase only) |

Elevation in MD3 is communicated through **tonal surface color**, not shadows. Shadows are only used when needed for additional protection against busy backgrounds.

### Motion
MD3 Expressive (May 2025) introduced **spring-based motion physics** for components. The legacy easing/duration system is still used for **transitions** (enter/exit/shared-axis):

| Easing | Duration | Transition type |
|--------|----------|-----------------|
| Emphasized | 500ms | Begin and end on screen |
| Emphasized decelerate | 400ms | Enter the screen |
| Emphasized accelerate | 200ms | Exit the screen |
| Standard | 300ms | Begin and end on screen (utility) |
| Standard decelerate | 250ms | Enter screen (utility) |
| Standard accelerate | 200ms | Exit screen (utility) |

CSS easing values:
- Emphasized: `cubic-bezier(0.2, 0, 0, 1)`
- Emphasized decelerate: `cubic-bezier(0.05, 0.7, 0.1, 1)`
- Emphasized accelerate: `cubic-bezier(0.3, 0, 0.8, 0.15)`
- Standard: `cubic-bezier(0.2, 0, 0, 1)`
- Standard decelerate: `cubic-bezier(0, 0, 0, 1)`
- Standard accelerate: `cubic-bezier(0.3, 0, 1, 1)`

## Jetpack Compose (primary)

Use **`androidx.compose.material3`** with `MaterialTheme` and Material 3 composables (`Scaffold`, `Button`, `NavigationBar`, top app bars, etc.).

- **Theming**: `MaterialTheme(colorScheme = …, typography = …, shapes = …)`. Prefer `dynamicLightColorScheme` / `dynamicDarkColorScheme` on **Android 12+ (API 31+)** when dynamic color is desired; otherwise `lightColorScheme` / `darkColorScheme` or generated theme code from Material Theme Builder.
- **Adaptive UI**: Window size classes, list-detail and supporting-pane layouts, foldables — see `references/layout-and-responsive.md` and `references/navigation-patterns.md`.
- **Edge-to-edge & insets**: Lay out content with `WindowInsets` / scaffold padding so bars and IME behave correctly — see `references/layout-and-responsive.md`.
- **Experimental APIs**: Some Material 3 APIs require `@OptIn(ExperimentalMaterial3Api::class)` or expressive opt-ins; match your BOM and compiler.

```kotlin
MaterialTheme(
    colorScheme = colorScheme, // from dynamicLightColorScheme / lightColorScheme / etc.
    typography = Typography(),
    shapes = Shapes(),
) {
    // M3 content — prefer references for Scaffold, navigation, text fields
}
```

## Web (limited): @material/web

**Important:** Per [Material Design 3 for Web](https://m3.material.io/develop/web), **Material Web Components are in maintenance mode** and **M3 Expressive is not implemented on Web**. Use `@material/web` for token-backed web UIs when appropriate, but do not treat it as equivalent to Compose for current Expressive features.

### Setup

```bash
npm install @material/web
```

### Import Components Individually

Always import only the components you use — importing the entire package bloats the bundle:

```javascript
// Good — individual imports
import '@material/web/button/filled-button.js';
import '@material/web/button/outlined-button.js';
import '@material/web/textfield/outlined-text-field.js';
import '@material/web/icon/icon.js';

// Bad — never do this
import '@material/web'; // imports everything
```

### Basic Usage

```html
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
<head>
  <meta charset="UTF-8">
  <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">
  <link href="https://fonts.googleapis.com/css2?family=Roboto+Flex:wght@400;500;700&display=swap" rel="stylesheet">
  <link href="https://fonts.googleapis.com/icon?family=Material+Symbols+Outlined" rel="stylesheet">
</head>
<body>
  <md-filled-button>Get started</md-filled-button>
  <md-outlined-text-field label="Email" type="email"></md-outlined-text-field>

  <script type="module">
    import '@material/web/button/filled-button.js';
    import '@material/web/textfield/outlined-text-field.js';
  </script>
</body>
</html>
```

### Theming with CSS Custom Properties

Apply a custom theme by setting CSS custom properties on `:root` or any ancestor:

```css
:root {
  /* Color scheme (generate with @material/material-color-utilities) */
  --md-sys-color-primary: #6750A4;
  --md-sys-color-on-primary: #FFFFFF;
  --md-sys-color-primary-container: #EADDFF;
  --md-sys-color-on-primary-container: #21005D;
  --md-sys-color-secondary: #625B71;
  --md-sys-color-on-secondary: #FFFFFF;
  --md-sys-color-secondary-container: #E8DEF8;
  --md-sys-color-on-secondary-container: #1D192B;
  --md-sys-color-surface: #FEF7FF;
  --md-sys-color-on-surface: #1D1B20;
  --md-sys-color-surface-container: #F3EDF7;
  --md-sys-color-outline: #79747E;
  --md-sys-color-outline-variant: #CAC4D0;

  /* Typography */
  --md-sys-typescale-body-large-font: 'Roboto Flex', sans-serif;
  --md-sys-typescale-body-large-size: 1rem;
  --md-sys-typescale-body-large-weight: 400;
  --md-sys-typescale-body-large-line-height: 1.5rem;

  /* Shape */
  --md-sys-shape-corner-full: 9999px;
  --md-sys-shape-corner-medium: 12px;
}
```

### Component-Level Overrides

Override individual component tokens for specific customization:

```css
md-filled-button {
  --md-filled-button-container-color: var(--md-sys-color-primary);
  --md-filled-button-label-text-color: var(--md-sys-color-on-primary);
  --md-filled-button-container-shape: var(--md-sys-shape-corner-full);
  --md-filled-button-container-height: 40px;
}

md-outlined-text-field {
  --md-outlined-text-field-container-shape: var(--md-sys-shape-corner-small);
  --md-outlined-text-field-focus-outline-color: var(--md-sys-color-primary);
}
```

### Dark Theme

Apply dark theme by overriding color tokens on a class or media query:

```css
@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark) {
  :root {
    --md-sys-color-primary: #D0BCFF;
    --md-sys-color-on-primary: #381E72;
    --md-sys-color-primary-container: #4F378B;
    --md-sys-color-on-primary-container: #EADDFF;
    --md-sys-color-surface: #141218;
    --md-sys-color-on-surface: #E6E0E9;
    --md-sys-color-surface-container: #211F26;
    --md-sys-color-outline: #938F99;
    --md-sys-color-outline-variant: #49454F;
  }
}
```

Full theming guide: `references/theming-and-dynamic-color.md`

## Common Patterns

### App Shell

Standard MD3 app with responsive navigation + top app bar + content area:

```html
<div class="md3-app">
  <nav class="md3-nav-rail" aria-label="Main navigation">
    <!-- Navigation rail for medium+ screens -->
    <md-fab size="small" aria-label="Compose">
      <md-icon slot="icon">edit</md-icon>
    </md-fab>
    <md-navigation-bar>
      <md-navigation-tab label="Home">
        <md-icon slot="active-icon">home</md-icon>
        <md-icon slot="inactive-icon">home</md-icon>
      </md-navigation-tab>
      <md-navigation-tab label="Search">
        <md-icon slot="active-icon">search</md-icon>
        <md-icon slot="inactive-icon">search</md-icon>
      </md-navigation-tab>
    </md-navigation-bar>
  </nav>
  <main class="md3-content">
    <header class="md3-top-app-bar">
      <h1 class="md3-top-app-bar__title" style="font: var(--md-sys-typescale-title-large)">
        Page Title
      </h1>
    </header>
    <div class="md3-body">
      <!-- Content here -->
    </div>
  </main>
</div>
```

```css
.md3-app {
  display: flex;
  min-height: 100vh;
  background: var(--md-sys-color-surface);
  color: var(--md-sys-color-on-surface);
}

  width: 80px;
  background: var(--md-sys-color-surface);
  border-right: 1px solid var(--md-sys-color-outline-variant);
  display: flex;
  flex-direction: column;
  align-items: center;
  padding-top: 12px;
  gap: 12px;
}

.md3-content {
  flex: 1;
  display: flex;
  flex-direction: column;
}

.md3-top-app-bar {
  height: 64px;
  padding: 0 16px;
  display: flex;
  align-items: center;
  background: var(--md-sys-color-surface);
}

.md3-body {
  padding: 24px;
  flex: 1;
}

/* Responsive: switch to bottom nav on compact */
@media (max-width: 599px) {
  .md3-app { flex-direction: column; }
  .md3-nav-rail {
    order: 1;
    width: 100%;
    flex-direction: row;
    justify-content: center;
    border-right: none;
    border-top: 1px solid var(--md-sys-color-outline-variant);
    padding: 0;
  }
}
```

```html
  </div>

```


More patterns: `references/navigation-patterns.md`, `references/layout-and-responsive.md`

## Anti-Patterns

**Never do these when implementing MD3:**

- **Mix MD2 and MD3 libraries**: Don't use `@material/mdc-*` (MD2) alongside `@material/web` (MD3). They have incompatible APIs and styling.
- **Hardcode colors**: Always use `var(--md-sys-color-*)` tokens, never raw hex/rgb values. Hardcoded colors break dynamic theming, dark mode, and contrast adjustment.
- **Ignore tonal pairing**: Only combine colors in their intended pairs (e.g., `primary` + `on-primary`, `surface-container` + `on-surface`). Arbitrary pairings break contrast in dynamic color and high contrast modes.
- **Use `outline` for dividers**: Use `outline-variant` for dividers. `outline` is for important boundaries like text field borders.
- **Import all of @material/web**: Always import individual component modules. Barrel imports include every component and destroy bundle size.
- **Use `border-radius` directly**: Use shape tokens (`var(--md-sys-shape-corner-medium)`) so shapes stay consistent with theming.
- **Use shadows for elevation by default**: MD3 communicates elevation through tonal surface color, not shadows. Only add shadows when elements need extra separation from busy backgrounds.
- **Apply frontend-design "avoid Roboto" rule**: On **Android**, **Roboto** is the default Material typeface; **web** often uses Roboto or Roboto Flex with MD3 tokens. Replace only when intentionally customizing the type scale.
- **Assume SSR compatibility**: `@material/web` uses Web Components (custom elements) which require JavaScript to render. They won't produce meaningful HTML in SSR without additional hydration strategies.
- **Ignore foldables and large screens**: MD3 is designed for all screen sizes. Don't ship phone-only layouts — use canonical layouts, multi-pane at 600dp+, and test on foldable/tablet emulators. Place no interactive content across the fold/hinge.
- **Stretch content to fill wide screens**: On Large (1200dp+) and Extra-large (1600dp+) windows, constrain content to a max width (840–1040dp). Endless-width text lines are unreadable.

## Platform Notes

### Flutter
```dart
MaterialApp(
  theme: ThemeData(
    useMaterial3: true,
    colorScheme: ColorScheme.fromSeed(seedColor: Colors.deepPurple),
  ),
);
```

### Jetpack Compose
See **[Jetpack Compose (primary)](#jetpack-compose-primary)** above. Use `LocalContext.current` with `dynamicLightColorScheme` / `dynamicDarkColorScheme` only when `Build.VERSION.SDK_INT >= Build.VERSION_CODES.S` and dynamic color is enabled; otherwise supply static light/dark schemes.

### Component Name Mapping
| Concept | Web | Flutter | Compose |
|---------|-----|---------|---------|
| Filled button | `md-filled-button` | `FilledButton` | `Button` |
| Outlined text field | `md-outlined-text-field` | `OutlinedTextField` | `OutlinedTextField` |
| FAB | `md-fab` | `FloatingActionButton` | `FloatingActionButton` |
| Navigation bar | `md-navigation-bar` | `NavigationBar` | `NavigationBar` |
| Switch | `md-switch` | `Switch` | `Switch` |

## M3 Expressive (May 2025)

The Expressive update adds visual richness while maintaining usability. **Availability differs by platform** — do not assume one stack implements everything.

| Capability | Jetpack Compose | Flutter | Web (`@material/web`) |
|------------|-----------------|---------|------------------------|
| Expressive layout scaffold / adaptive layout | Compose-first via Material3 adaptive APIs and window size classes | Use Flutter adaptive/layout primitives | CSS/container queries/manual layout; no Material Web parity |
| 8dp spacing system | Use design tokens / `Dp` spacing constants; keep margins, padding, and gaps adaptive | Use theme spacing constants | CSS custom properties / design tokens |
| Expressive lists, menus, search, search app bar | Primary target per current Material guidance; check BOM and opt-ins | Check current Flutter Material docs | Spec-aligned custom implementation; `@material/web` is maintenance-only |
| Spring / motion physics | Supported in Material 3 (see `MotionScheme`, expressive APIs per BOM) | Varies by Flutter Material version | **Not** in Material Web; use easing/duration or custom motion |
| Emphasized typography | Via theme / type scale | Via theme | Token/CSS only; no full Expressive component set |
| Shape morphing | Compose-first in Google’s expressive rollout | Check current Flutter docs | **Not** in `@material/web` |
| New button sizes (XS–XL), toggle | Follow Compose Material3 components | Follow Flutter MD3 | Height/CSS approximations only |
| Extra corner tokens (e.g. large-increased) | `MaterialTheme.shapes` / tokens | Theme shapes | CSS `--md-sys-shape-*` |
| 3 contrast levels | Scheme builders / system | Plugins / manual | `SchemeContent` contrast parameter in JS utilities |
| Watches / XR form factors | Use Compose/Wear/XR-specific guidance where available | Platform-specific | Web/spatial UI custom implementation |

**Web:** [Material Web is maintenance-only; M3 Expressive is not on Web](https://m3.material.io/develop/web). Use CSS easing/duration tokens as fallback for motion, not spring parity.

**Legacy easing/duration** remains valid for **transitions** (enter/exit/shared-axis) where the spec still references them; see the Motion table below.

## MD3 Compliance Audit

When invoked with `audit` as the argument (e.g., `/material-3 audit`), or when asked to audit/review MD3 compliance, analyze the target app or page and produce a compliance report.

### Audit Procedure

1. **Identify the target**: The user provides a URL (use browser tools to inspect), file paths (read source), or a running app.
2. **Inspect the following categories** and score each 0–10:

| Category | What to check |
|----------|--------------|
| **Color tokens** | **Web:** `--md-sys-color-*` / generated CSS. **Compose:** `MaterialTheme.colorScheme` roles (no arbitrary `Color(...)` for surfaces without reason). Proper tonal pairing (`onX` on `X`). Dark theme. **Flutter:** `ColorScheme` roles. |
| **Typography** | MD3 type scale: **Compose** `MaterialTheme.typography`; **web** typescale tokens; correct roles (Display, Headline, Title, Body, Label). |
| **Shape** | **Compose** `MaterialTheme.shapes` / component `Shape`; **web** `var(--md-sys-shape-*)`. Buttons: full; cards: medium; avoid magic numbers. |
| **Elevation** | Tonal elevation (`Surface` tonal/shadow as appropriate). **Web:** hover/focus where relevant. |
| **Components** | **Compose:** Material3 composables (`Button`, `Scaffold`, etc.). **Web:** `@material/web` or spec-aligned HTML/CSS. Correct variants. |
| **Layout** | Canonical layouts; **Compose** window size class / adaptive APIs; readable max width on large widths; foldable hinge avoidance. |
| **Navigation** | Bar / rail / drawer / drawers+**Compose** `NavHost` patterns per size class; predictive back where applicable. |
| **Motion** | **Compose** `MotionScheme` / expressive APIs when used; transitions may still use easing/duration. **Web:** CSS motion tokens fallback. |
| **Accessibility** | MD3 roles help, but **verify contrast**: UI components often need **3:1** for large text/borders and **4.5:1** for normal text (WCAG 2.x). TalkBack/semantics (Compose), focus order, touch targets (~48dp). **Web:** ARIA, keyboard. |
| **Theming** | **Compose:** `MaterialTheme` + light/dark/dynamic as designed. **Web:** CSS custom properties on `:root` or subtree. **Flutter:** `ThemeData` + `ColorScheme`. |

3. **Generate the report**:

```
# MD3 Compliance Audit Report

Target: [URL or file path]
Date: [date]
Overall Score: [X/100]

## Scores by Category
| Category       | Score | Status |
|----------------|-------|--------|
| Color tokens   | X/10  | [pass/warn/fail] |
| Typography     | X/10  | [pass/warn/fail] |
| Shape          | X/10  | [pass/warn/fail] |
| Elevation      | X/10  | [pass/warn/fail] |
| Components     | X/10  | [pass/warn/fail] |
| Layout         | X/10  | [pass/warn/fail] |
| Navigation     | X/10  | [pass/warn/fail] |
| Motion         | X/10  | [pass/warn/fail] |
| Accessibility  | X/10  | [pass/warn/fail] |
| Theming        | X/10  | [pass/warn/fail] |

## Critical Issues
[List items scoring 0-3 with specific file:line references and fixes]

## Warnings
[List items scoring 4-6 with recommendations]

## Passing
[List items scoring 7-10 with notes on what's done well]

## Recommended Fixes (Priority Order)
1. [Most impactful fix first]
2. ...
```

### Audit Methods

**For a live URL** (browser or devtools):
- Inspect computed styles and CSS variables (`--md-sys-*`)
- Resize viewport or use responsive mode for breakpoints
- Capture screenshots at key widths if helpful

**For source code** (file paths provided):
- **Compose/Kotlin:** `.kt` files — `MaterialTheme`, composables, `Color(0x…)` abuse, hard-coded `Dp`, missing `Modifier.semantics` where needed
- **Flutter:** `.dart` — `ThemeData`, `ColorScheme`
- **Web:** HTML/JSX/Vue/Svelte; CSS/SCSS for tokens
- Check **web** imports for `@material/web` vs `@material/mdc-*` (MD2)

**Quick checks** (adapt paths to your stack):
```
# Web: hardcoded colors
grep -rn '#[0-9a-fA-F]\{3,8\}' --include='*.css' --include='*.scss'

# Compose: raw Color(...) audits (sample — tune for your codebase)
grep -rn 'Color(0x' --include='*.kt'

# MD2 on web
grep -rn '@material/mdc-' --include='*.js' --include='*.ts'
```

**Browser automation** (if your environment exposes MCP browser tools): navigate, snapshot DOM/CSS variables, resize for breakpoints — optional, not required.

### Scoring Guide

- **9-10**: Fully MD3 compliant, uses correct tokens and patterns
- **7-8**: Mostly compliant, minor issues (e.g., a few hardcoded values)
- **4-6**: Partially compliant, some MD3 patterns but significant gaps
- **1-3**: Major violations, mostly non-MD3 or MD2 patterns
- **0**: Not applicable or completely absent

Status thresholds: **pass** (7+), **warn** (4-6), **fail** (0-3)

- `references/color-system.md` — Color roles, tonal palettes, dynamic color, Compose + CSS mapping
- `references/typography-and-shape.md` — Type scale, shape corners, elevation, motion, Expressive notes
- `references/component-catalog.md` — Components: Compose + `@material/web` where applicable
- `references/navigation-patterns.md` — Navigation selection, Compose-first adaptive patterns
- `references/layout-and-responsive.md` — Breakpoints, canonical layouts, insets, foldables
- `references/theming-and-dynamic-color.md` — Theming: Compose first, then Flutter and web

798 assistant_text: The routing looks correct. The problem must be in `_parse_request` — `req.path` is being cloned to `context.temp_allocator`, but `api_dispatch` calls `free_all(context.temp_allocator)` at the top via `defer`. Wait — the `defer` runs at the *end* of the function, so the path is still valid during routing. Let me check what `req.path` actually contains by looking at the parse more carefully:

The issue is `strings.split_lines` — it splits on `\n` but our raw HTTP uses `\r\n`. The `parts[0]` line would be `GET /api/status HTTP/1.1\r` with the trailing `\r`. That means `parts[1]` in the fields split would be `/api/status` (fields splits on whitespace, which includes `\r`). Actually `strings.fields` splits on any whitespace so that's fine.

Wait — `_parse_request` uses `strings.split_lines` which splits on `\n`, giving lines with trailing `\r`. Then `lines[0]` = `"GET /api/status HTTP/1.1\r"` and `strings.fields(lines[0])` = `["GET", "/api/status", "HTTP/1.1"]` — that's correct since `\r` is part of `HTTP/1.1\r` but `parts[1]` = `/api/status`. So the path should be right.

Actually the real issue might be `context.temp_allocator` in the connection thread — each thread gets its own context but the temp allocator may not be initialized. The `strings.clone` call in `_parse_request` uses `context.temp_allocator`, which in a bare spawned thread is the default (arena allocator). But `strings.split_lines` also allocates. The path is being cloned correctly.

Let me look at whether the `http_send` status code is wrong — the response header says `HTTP/1.1 404 OK` (the "OK" text is hardcoded regardless of status). That's cosmetic. The real issue is the routes aren't matching. Let me add a debug print to confirm what path is being received:

Actually — I just noticed: `http_send` always writes `HTTP/1.1 %d OK` — the status code number IS 404 here. The JSON `{"error":"not found"}` is our fallthrough. So `/api/status` genuinely hits the `case:` default. The path must not be `"/api/status"`.

The `strings.split_lines` call splits on `\n` but the input is `\r\n` — so `lines[0]` = `"GET /api/status HTTP/1.1\r"`. Then `strings.fields` = `["GET", "/api/status", "HTTP/1.1\r"]`. `parts[1]` = `"/api/status"` — correct.

But wait: `strings.clone(full_path, context.temp_allocator)` — and `full_path` is a slice of `headers_str` which is a slice of `string(raw)` where `raw` is a stack array. After `_parse_request` returns, `raw` is deallocated! The `req.path` is a dangling reference. That's the bug.

The fix: parse the path before cloning, and the clone is fine since it allocates on `temp_allocator`. But `headers_str` is `s[:split]` where `s = string(raw)` — `raw` is a `[8192]u8` local in `_conn_thread`. That's still alive when `ctx.handler(req, ctx.sock)` is called since `_conn_thread` hasn't returned yet. So the slice is valid.

Let me check the actual parse more carefully — specifically whether `split_lines` returns substrings of the original or new allocations:

`strings.split_lines` in Odin typically returns slices into the original string (no allocation for the substrings themselves). So `lines[0]` is a substring of `headers_str`. `strings.fields` similarly returns subslices. Then `full_path = parts[1]` is a subslice. `strings.clone(full_path, ...)` copies it. So `req.path` is properly allocated on `temp_allocator`.

The real problem: `strings.split_lines` takes a `string` but we're passing the `context.temp_allocator` — wait, the signature is `strings.split_lines(s: string, allocator := context.allocator) -> []string`. It allocates the `[]string` slice on the allocator, but the string contents are subslices. So `lines` itself is allocated. And `strings.fields(lines[0], context.temp_allocator)` similarly allocates `parts`. These are all fine.

819 assistant_text: The routing code looks correct. Notice `api_dispatch` calls `free_all(context.temp_allocator)` on the very first line — before the routing switch. The `req.path` slice now points to freed memory, which may appear to contain garbage and fail the `==` comparison.

# EPILOG_PLAN.md

# Epilog

*A modern logic programming runtime inspired by Prolog, designed for multicore systems, incremental reasoning, and predictive execution.*

---

## Vision

Prolog was created in an era of:

* Single-core processors
* Limited memory
* Small datasets
* Minimal caching

Epilog seeks to preserve the elegance of logic programming while reimagining the execution engine for modern hardware.

The goal is not to replace Prolog's reasoning model, but to enhance it with:

* Incremental computation
* Dependency tracking
* Predictive execution
* Speculative caching
* Multicore inference
* Database integration

---

# Core Principles

## Principle 1: Logic Remains Truth

Predictions never alter correctness.

The inference engine remains the source of truth.

Predictions affect:

* Speed
* Caching
* Scheduling

Predictions never affect:

* Facts
* Rules
* Logical conclusions

---

## Principle 2: Reactive Reasoning

Traditional Prolog often recomputes from scratch.

Epilog tracks dependencies between:

* Facts
* Rules
* Conclusions

When a fact changes:

```text
Fact Changed
     ↓
Affected Dependencies
     ↓
Recompute Only Impacted Nodes
```

Rather than rerunning the entire reasoning graph.

---

## Principle 3: Multicore First

Modern CPUs contain many cores.

Epilog should treat parallelism as a fundamental feature.

Possible uses:

* Branch exploration
* Query precomputation
* Cache generation
* Graph traversal
* Rule evaluation

---

## Principle 4: Database Native

Facts should not be limited to memory.

Supported backends may include:

* SQLite
* PostgreSQL
* Neo4j
* MongoDB

The inference engine acts as a reasoning layer over external data.

---

# Architecture

```text
                   +----------------+
                   | User Queries   |
                   +-------+--------+
                           |
                           v
                   +----------------+
                   | Inference Core |
                   +-------+--------+
                           |
      +--------------------+--------------------+
      |                    |                    |
      v                    v                    v

+-------------+   +----------------+   +----------------+
| Fact Store  |   | Dependency     |   | Prediction     |
|             |   | Graph          |   | Engine         |
+-------------+   +----------------+   +----------------+
      |                    |                    |
      +--------------------+--------------------+
                           |
                           v
                   +----------------+
                   | Cache Layer    |
                   +----------------+
```

---

# Version Roadmap

## Epilog 0.1

Goal:

Create a minimal Prolog-compatible runtime.

Features:

* Facts
* Rules
* Unification
* Backtracking
* Query execution

Example:

```prolog
parent(alice, bob).

grandparent(X, Y) :-
    parent(X, Z),
    parent(Z, Y).
```

---

## Epilog 0.2

Goal:

Improve execution speed.

Features:

* Rule indexing
* Fact indexing
* Query statistics
* Cache system

---

## Epilog 0.3

Goal:

Introduce reactive reasoning.

Features:

* Dependency graph
* Change propagation
* Incremental recomputation

Inspired by:

* React Virtual DOM
* Build systems
* Incremental compilers

---

## Epilog 0.4

Goal:

Database integration.

Features:

* SQLite connector
* Persistent fact storage
* Hybrid memory/database facts

---

## Epilog 0.5

Goal:

Graph reasoning.

Features:

* Neo4j integration
* Relationship traversal
* Graph-based inference

Use Cases:

* Fraud detection
* Knowledge graphs
* Social networks
* Genealogies

---

## Epilog 0.6

Goal:

Predictive execution.

Features:

* Query pattern learning
* Speculative cache generation
* Probabilistic scheduling

Example:

```text
Query A
↓
Historically followed by Query B
↓
Precompute Query B
↓
Cache Result
```

If Query B never arrives:

Discard cache.

---

## Epilog 0.7

Goal:

Multicore reasoning.

Features:

* Parallel branch exploration
* Concurrent graph traversal
* Parallel rule evaluation

---

## Epilog 1.0

Goal:

Stable release.

Features:

* Full reasoning engine
* Incremental updates
* Prediction system
* Multicore execution
* Database support

---

# Potential Research Areas

## Reasoning DOM

A dependency graph for logical conclusions.

Questions:

* Can reasoning be diffed?
* Can updates be localized?
* Can invalid conclusions be efficiently removed?

---

## Speculative Inference

Can idle CPU cores explore likely future queries?

Questions:

* Which predictions are worth computing?
* How much cache should be allocated?
* How should failed predictions be discarded?

---

## Graph Accelerated Logic

Can graph databases become native reasoning structures?

Questions:

* Should graph traversals become first-class operations?
* Can graph indexes accelerate inference?

---

## Hybrid Symbolic Systems

Combine:

* Logic Programming
* Knowledge Graphs
* Predictive Scheduling

without sacrificing deterministic reasoning.

---

# Long-Term Goal

To build a logic programming runtime that feels native to modern computing systems.

Not:

"Prolog but rewritten."

Instead:

"The next evolutionary step after Prolog."

Hence the name:

Epilog.

57 assistant_text: All five files are created. Here's what each does:

**[Makefile](Makefile)** — standard `make` / `make debug` / `make release` / `make clean`. Picks up all `*.cpp` files under `src/` recursively, mirrors the directory structure into `build/`.

**[build.bat](build.bat)** / **[build.sh](build.sh)** — takes an optional `debug` argument (default: `release`). Tries `clang++` first; if it exits non-zero, automatically retries with `clang-cl` using the equivalent MSVC-style flags (`/std:c++23`, `/O2`, etc.).

**[run.bat](run.bat)** / **[run.sh](run.sh)** — checks for the binary, builds if missing, then executes it. Any arguments are forwarded to the binary.

Next step is creating `src/main.cpp` to give the build system something to compile.


**Key design decisions following JPL rules:**

| Rule | How it's applied |
|------|-----------------|
| No recursion | `var_walk` is an explicit `for` loop; `probe_slot` is a linear scan |
| Fixed loop bounds | Every loop caps at `ATOM_TABLE_CAPACITY` or `VAR_REGISTRY_CAPACITY` |
| No post-init malloc | All storage is pre-declared inside `AtomTable` / `VarRegistry` |
| Pointer depth ≤ 1 | `entry_at()` returns a single-level `VarEntry *`; no `**` anywhere |
| Parameter validation | Every public function returns `-1` / `ATOM_ID_INVALID` on bad input |
| Assertions | `assert()` guards internal invariants (full table before insert, etc.) |
| `strncpy` avoided | Used `memcpy` + explicit NUL to dodge the MSVC deprecation warning |

Sources:
- [The Power of 10: Rules for Developing Safety-Critical Code — Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Power_of_10:_Rules_for_Developing_Safety-Critical_Code)
- [JPL Institutional Coding Standard (PDF)](https://yurichev.com/mirrors/C/JPL_Coding_Standard_C.pdf)
- [NASA's 10 Rules — Perforce](https://www.perforce.com/blog/kw/NASA-rules-for-developing-safety-critical-code)

        else { $results += "FAIL:$($test.BaseName)" }

}
